They're that sort of shiny plastic material that cracks if you bend it too much. But they do the job. I used to wear real leather belts back when I was younger. Had this beautiful hand-tooled one with a silver buckle. Bought it at a leather shop down in New Mexico when my wife and I took a road trip out west in the seventies. Excessively? No, no, I don't think so. I mean, you asked me this earlier, didn't you? About worrying when there's no reason? It's the same thing, really. I don't fret over every little thing. There's enough real things to handle without making up new ones.

Had this beautiful hand-tooled one with a silver buckle. Bought it at a leather shop down in New Mexico when my wife and I took a road trip out west in the seventies. We drove this old Ford station wagon with the wood paneling on the side. The air conditioning broke somewhere in Texas, and we had to drive with the windows down all the way to Albuquerque. The wind noise was so loud you couldn't even hear the radio. Not that we could get any good stations out there anyway, just static and farm reports...\n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I lost my train of thought. What were we talking about? Was it the road trip?\n\nDoctor: Do you often fear that something bad is going to happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Fear that something bad is going to happen? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't sit around waiting for the sky to fall, if that's what you mean. I just take things one day at a time. \n\nMy neighbor, though, Mrs. Higgins, she's a different story. She's always convinced a hurricane is coming, even in January. She keeps her pantry stocked with those giant cans of baked beans and peaches. You know, the industrial-sized ones they use in school cafeterias? I helped her carry some in from her car last week. Boy, those things are heavy. They come in these big cardboard flats, wrapped in that thick plastic shrink wrap that you can never quite tear open with your fingers. You always need a pocket knife. I used to carry a Swiss Army knife everywhere I went. The red one with the little toothpick and tweezers tucked into the handle. I lost the tweezers years ago, probably trying to pull a splinter out of my thumb when I was building the deck out back. I used treated pine for that deck. The guy at the lumber yard said it would last twenty years, but it started rotting after ten. Had to tear the whole thing down and start over. I rented one of those big crowbars, a pry bar, I think they call it. The thing was solid iron, painted bright yellow...\n\nI'm sorry, I've gone off track again, haven't I? There's enough real things to handle without making up new ones. \n\nLike the boxes at work. I'm a stocker down at the warehouse, you know. I just turned 74, but I still go in. I used to use this special soap to get it off, Boraxo, I think it was called. Came in a cardboard can with a metal spout...\n\nWait, hold on. I'm sorry, doctor. I'm doing it again. Anxious for no reason? Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. Like I just said a minute ago, no. I don't get worked up over nothing. I just go to work, do my job, eat my lunch, and come home. 

Speaking of lunch, they actually took the microwave out of the break room at the warehouse on Tuesday. Said it was a fire hazard because somebody burned a bag of popcorn in it. Now I have to bring my soup in a thermos if I want it hot. I've got this old Stanley thermos, the heavy green metal kind. I think I bought it at a hardware store back in '92. They really don't make them like that anymore. The new ones are all plastic and they don't keep the heat past ten in the morning. My wife used to make this chicken noodle soup from scratch to put in there, with the thick egg noodles. She'd roll the dough out right on the kitchen counter, get flour all over the floor. We had this linoleum flooring back then, sort of a pale yellow with little flecks of brown in it. It was supposed to look like stone, I guess, but it just looked like dirty linoleum. I took a mop to it every Sunday with Pine-Sol. The smell of Pine-Sol always reminds me of Sunday mornings. We'd listen to the radio while I mopped...

Oh, for heaven's sake, I'm rambling again. I'm sorry, my mind just wanders off. What were we talking about? Was it the microwave?

Just worn completely smooth from handling all those boxes and packing tape for years. My wife used to joke that I had hands like fine-grit sandpaper. Fifty dollars was a lot of money back then, let me tell you. I was working at a gas station at the time, pumping gas and checking oil. Full service, they called it. You don't see that anymore. Now you freeze your hands off in the winter trying to get the pump to work...\n\nWait, I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Right before? Well, before all of this, I was keeping my routine and my responsibilities together just fine. I had my schedule, and I stuck to it. But then the warehouse changed my hours and brought in that new inventory system I was telling you about. It was within the first few weeks after they did all that when I really just... started coming apart. 

It was the new scanners for that system that really threw me. They look like little plastic phaser guns from Star Trek. I used to watch Star Trek every Thursday night. The original one, you know, with William Shatner and Leonard Nimoy. We had this heavy wooden console television set back then. It was a Zenith, I think. Real wood veneer on the sides, with the speakers built right into the front covered by that scratchy cloth. The thing weighed an absolute ton and took up half the wall in the den. You had to physically get up off the couch and turn this big clunky dial if you wanted to change the channel. Not that there was much to change to, just channels 3, 5, and 8. Sometimes we'd try to get the UHF channels, but you had to mess with the rabbit ear antennas for twenty minutes. I used to wrap aluminum foil on the tips to get a better signal so we could watch the baseball games. My wife absolutely hated the foil. She said it made the living room look like a junkyard. She was very particular about how the house looked, always keeping the vacuum tracks perfectly straight on the carpet. She had this heavy old Hoover upright vacuum with a headlight on the front of it... 

Oh, Lord, there I go again. I'm so sorry, doctor. I completely lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Something about the warehouse schedule?

Back when I took a few classes at the community college in the seventies, they didn't have computers anything like this. Everything was punch cards. My buddy Earl used to carry a stack of them in his front shirt pocket, and one time he dropped the whole lot of them right in a parking lot puddle... absolute disaster, took us three days to sort it out. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Right, the new system at work. It's just... I can't keep up, and I'm missing my shifts now. I'm even missing my church commitments, which isn't like me. They last forever if you remember to oil the leather right before winter. But lately I just sit there in those boots, staring at the floor, wondering if I can even face going into that place anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Enjoy things? I'm... I'm not really sure. I haven't really thought about it. I used to like the Sunday potlucks down at the parish, I guess. My wife used to make this incredible green bean casserole for them. The trick, she always said, was soaking the onions in buttermilk first. I actually tried making it myself a couple of years ago for Thanksgiving, but I left them in the oil too long and the whole house smelled like burnt grease for a solid week. James Arness, now he was a giant of a man. My brother-in-law, Frank, actually met him once at a grocery store out in California. \n\nFrank moved out to Fresno back in '82 because he thought he was going to start a landscaping business. He bought this old Chevy truck that just kept breaking down on him. I told him, I said, Frank, you gotta check the transmission on those older models, but he didn't listen, and the whole thing just dropped right out on the highway. Cost him an absolute fortune to tow it. He ended up having to move back here and sleep in my basement for six months, eating us out of house and home... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you ask me about Frank?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Nervous? On edge? Well, I wouldn't say that exactly. It's more like... I just can't settle down, you know? Like my brain is always buzzing. It reminds me of this old refrigerator we used to have down in the basement. An old Frigidaire, from the fifties, I think. Heavy as a tank. The motor on that thing would just hum all day and night. Bzzzzz. You'd be upstairs trying to watch the evening news—Walter Cronkite, you know, the most trusted man in America—and you could still hear that fridge just grinding away right through the floorboards. \n\nMy wife kept saying we needed to get rid of it, but I told her, 'Mary, they don't make them like this anymore! It keeps the beer ice cold!' I used to keep a case of Pabst Blue Ribbon down there. Me and Earl would sit on these rickety old folding chairs next to the washing machine, just drinking Pabst and listening to the ballgame on the radio. The Cardinals were really something back then. Bob Gibson on the mound... what an arm. He pitched a whole game with a broken leg once, did you know that? Just taped it up and kept throwing heat. You don't see toughness like that nowadays. Kids today, they stub their toe and they want a week off work... \n\nI'm sorry, what was the question? Something about the refrigerator?\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about things that are coming up or things that might happen?\n\nPatient: Worry about things coming up? I don't... I don't really know if I worry, exactly. It's not like I sit around biting my nails. But my mind does wander. It's like when I used to work the night shift at the warehouse, stocking shelves. You'd be pulling pallets of canned goods—mostly soup, Campbell's, you know, chicken noodle, tomato. The tomato was always the heaviest for some reason. Anyway, you'd be in this massive aisle, just you and the hum of the forklift, and your mind would just start drifting to all sorts of things. \n\nI had this supervisor back then, a guy named Stan. Stan was a real piece of work. He used to wear these bright yellow suspenders every single day. Said they were lucky. He bought them at a flea market out near the state line. That flea market was incredible, actually. They sold everything from antique pocket watches to live chickens. I bought a set of socket wrenches there once for five bucks, and I still use them. Craftsman, back when they actually meant something. I tried to fix my neighbor's lawnmower with them last spring, but the spark plug was completely fused... \n\nWait, I'm sorry. Did you ask me about the warehouse?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time?\n\nPatient: Anxious most of the time? I... well, I don't know about \"most of the time.\" It's hard to put a label on it. It just feels like I'm waiting for the other shoe to drop, you know? Like when you're waiting for the mail carrier to bring a package. Our old mailman, Gary, he used to come around at exactly 11:15 every morning. You could set your watch by him. He drove one of those little white trucks with the steering wheel on the wrong side. \n\nI always wondered how they learn to drive those things. Must be confusing, sitting on the right side. Like driving in England. My cousin Arthur went to England back in '95. Or was it '96? He brought me back this little souvenir double-decker bus. Cast iron, real heavy. I kept it on the mantle for years until the cat knocked it off and chipped the paint. That cat was a menace, a big orange tabby named Marmalade. He used to bring dead mice and leave them right on the welcome mat. My wife would scream bloody murder every time she opened the front door...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Did you ask about Gary the mailman?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most days?\n\nPatient: Sad most days? I don't... I wouldn't say I'm sad, really. It's more just a heaviness, I guess. Like dragging around a wet wool blanket. My grandfather had a wool blanket just like that, an old army issue one from when he served in Korea. Olive drab green, scratchy as all get out. He used to keep it folded at the foot of his bed, right next to this cedar chest my grandmother bought at a Sears Roebuck catalog. \n\nYou remember those catalogs? They used to be thick as a phone book. You could buy anything out of them—clothes, tools, even whole houses. Kit houses, they called them. They'd ship all the lumber and nails right on a train, and you'd build it yourself. There's a house down on Elm Street, the blue one with the wrap-around porch? That's a Sears house. The Johnsons lived there for forty years. Good people, the Johnsons. Mr. Johnson grew the most incredible tomatoes I've ever seen. Beefsteaks, big as softballs. He swore his secret was using crushed eggshells in the soil. I tried it once in my own garden, but the raccoons just dug everything up...\n\nWait, I'm sorry. Did you ask me about the garden?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because of how they make you feel?\n\nPatient: Avoiding places? I don't think I do that on purpose. I just... I mostly stay home now. It's easier, you know? Not having to deal with the traffic. The traffic on Route 9 has gotten so bad lately. They put in that new traffic light by the strip mall, and it backs up for miles. \n\nI remember when that strip mall was just an empty field. Used to go out there with my buddy Carl and launch model rockets. The Estes kind, with the little solid propellant engines. We'd spend all Saturday gluing the fins on, painting them up real nice. Carl was always better at painting than I was. He had this steady hand. Ended up becoming a dentist, actually. Moved out to Arizona, somewhere near Tucson. Sent me a postcard once with a picture of a saguaro cactus on it. Those things can live for over a hundred years, you know. Just standing out there in the desert, soaking up the sun. I always thought it'd be nice to visit the desert, see the Grand Canyon. My wife and I talked about taking an RV trip out there when I retired, but we never got around to buying the RV. They're so expensive, and the gas mileage is terrible...\n\nI'm sorry, what was the question? Did you ask about Carl?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Lost weight? No, I don't think so. My pants still fit about the same, maybe a little loose around the waist, but I've had these slacks for years. Bought them at JCPenney back when they still had a store in the mall. The mall is practically a ghost town now. It's a shame, really. I used to love going there on a Saturday morning, getting a pretzel from that little stand near the fountain. \n\nThey had this big fountain right in the center, with all these pennies at the bottom. Kids would throw coins in and make wishes. I wonder what happens to all those coins when they clean the fountain. Do they donate it to charity? Or does the mall just keep it? I read an article once about how much money they pull out of the Trevi Fountain in Rome. Millions of euros, they said. Can you imagine? Just scooping up all that money. I've always wanted to go to Italy, see the Colosseum, eat real pasta. My mother used to make spaghetti on Sundays, but she'd boil the noodles until they were practically mush. She meant well, bless her heart, but she was never much of a cook. Her meatloaf was like a brick. We used to joke you could use it to build a retaining wall...\n\nWait, did you ask me about my mother's cooking? I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're no longer enjoying things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things? I... I don't know. I guess I haven't really been doing much of anything lately to know if I enjoy it or not. I used to like bowling. I was in a league on Thursday nights down at Pinewood Lanes. We had a good team, too. The Pinbusters, we called ourselves. We even had matching shirts made up. Blue with white collars. \n\nMy buddy Frank, he was the anchor. He could throw a hook that would just curve right into the pocket every time. It was a beautiful thing to watch. He used a 16-pound ball, Brunswick, black as pitch. He polished that thing like it was a classic car. Speaking of classic cars, there was a guy in our neighborhood who restored an old '57 Chevy Bel Air. Cherry red with the white fins. He'd spend every weekend out in his driveway, buffing the chrome. I always wanted a car like that. Had a Ford Pinto once, back in the seventies. Terrible car. The heater never worked right, and you always felt like you were taking your life in your hands if you got rear-ended. Remember that? They had to recall them because the gas tanks would explode. My wife made me sell it. She was always practical like that. She kept all our receipts in these little manila envelopes, categorized by month...\n\nI'm sorry, what did you ask me? Did you want to know about the bowling league?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? I don't really think so. I wouldn't use the word empty. I fill orders over at the warehouse, you know, and we just end up with absolute mountains of empty cardboard boxes. \n\nThe cardboard baler is always breaking down on us. The maintenance guy, Jimmy, he's supposed to fix it, but he usually just kicks the side of the machine and slaps a piece of duct tape on the safety latch. Jimmy used to be in the Navy. Stationed out in San Diego, he told me once. He's got this anchor tattoo on his forearm, but it just looks like a blurry blue blob now. I guess tattoos do that when you get older, the ink just kind of spreads out under your skin. My uncle Arthur had a hula girl on his bicep, and by the time he was my age, it looked like a head of cabbage. He was a real character, Arthur. Played the accordion. He used to play at these big Polish weddings on the weekends, polka music, everyone dancing and sweating... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Did you ask about my uncle?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going back to work?\n\nPatient: Anxious about work? Yeah, I guess you could say that. I'm missing my shifts, I haven't been to my church commitments in weeks, and I'm snapping at my wife and kids. Some mornings I just sit there on the edge of the bed in my work boots, staring at the floor, wondering if I can even face the place anymore. \n\nThose work boots, they're Red Wings. Good, sturdy boots. I bought them at this little shoe store downtown that's been there since the fifties. The guy who runs it, Mr. Henderson, he still uses one of those old metal foot measurers, you know the ones? The Brannock device, I think it's called. You step on it and he slides the little metal pieces to check your width and length. Nobody does that anymore. Now you just go to a big box store and guess your size. It's a real shame. Mr. Henderson always throws in a free pair of wool socks with every purchase, too. Gray wool with a red stripe at the top. They're great for the winter, keep your toes nice and toasty. I tried wearing them in the summer once, though, and my feet sweat so much I thought I was going to slide right out of my boots...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you ask about my boots?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself staying away from certain situations or activities because you're afraid of failing or making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Afraid of making mistakes? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really avoid things because of that. I mean, everybody makes mistakes, right? Like last week, I was trying to fix the leaky faucet in the downstairs bathroom. Just a simple washer replacement, or so I thought. I went to the hardware store—the one on Elm Street, not the big Home Depot out on the highway. The guy at the local place, he knows his stuff. He's got these little bins filled with every kind of screw and washer you could ever imagine. \n\nAnyway, I bought the wrong size washer. Brought it home, took the whole faucet apart, and realized it didn't fit. Water was spraying everywhere. I had to run down to the basement to shut off the main valve. The basement always smells a little musty, especially after it rains. We had a sump pump put in back in '98 when we got that terrible flood. The water came right up to the bottom step. Ruined a bunch of old photo albums my wife had stored in cardboard boxes. She was devastated. You know, you really shouldn't store things in cardboard if there's any chance of water. Plastic bins are the way to go. They have those ones with the snap-on lids now, very secure. \n\nI'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What were you asking me about? Was it the plumbing?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things at work even when you're not there?\n\nPatient: Worrying about work when I'm at home? No, I wouldn't say I do that. Once I clock out, it's out of my mind. Speaking of clocking out, we actually still use those old paper punch cards at the warehouse. You slide it in the slot and it goes *ka-chunk*, you know? It's a very satisfying sound. \n\nThey keep talking about bringing in one of those fancy fingerprint scanners, but I don't know how that would work for me. My fingerprints are practically gone. Just worn completely smooth from handling all those boxes and packing tape for years. My wife used to joke that I had hands like fine-grit sandpaper. She bought me this expensive hand cream once to soften them up. It came in this heavy glass pump bottle, smelled like... I think it was lavender and oatmeal? Anyway, I knocked the darn thing off the bathroom sink the second day I had it. Shattered everywhere. Getting glass out of tile grout is a nightmare, let me tell you. I had to drag the heavy Hoover vacuum upstairs. It's one of those old metal ones from the eighties, weighs an absolute ton, but it could suck a golf ball through a garden hose. Not like the cheap plastic ones they make now... \n\nI'm sorry, I completely lost my train of thought. Were you asking about my vacuum?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Worrying when there's no reason? No, not really. I mean, if there's nothing to worry about, I don't worry. I've always been pretty level-headed that way, you know? Like my grandfather used to say, don't borrow trouble. \n\nHe was a smart man, my grandfather. Worked on the railroad for forty-five years. He had this beautiful pocket watch, a Hamilton. Gold-plated, with the Roman numerals on the face. He used to let me wind it when I was a kid. You had to be real gentle with the stem, just turn it until you felt a little resistance. If you over-wound it, you'd snap the mainspring, and then you'd have to take it down to the jeweler on Main Street. Mr. Peterson, I think his name was. He wore this little magnifying glass on his eye—a loupe, they call it. Looked like a monocle. He had hands as steady as a surgeon. He fixed my wife's engagement ring once when a prong got bent. It's a modest ring, just a small diamond, but she loves it. We picked it out together at a pawn shop back in '71. Paid fifty dollars for it. Fifty dollars was a lot of money back then, let me tell you. I was working at a gas station at the time, pumping gas and checking oil. Full service, they called it. You don't see that anymore. Now you freeze your hands off in the winter trying to get the pump to work...\n\nWait, I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Was it about my grandfather?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight change? Hmm, no, I haven't really noticed anything like that. My clothes still fit about the same, I suppose. I buy mostly those trousers with the elastic in the waistband now anyway. They're just more comfortable when you're bending and lifting boxes all day. \n\nI actually get them from this catalog that comes in the mail. It's called Haband, I think. Was it the road trip?\n\nDoctor: Do you often fear that something bad is going to happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Fear that something bad is going to happen? No, I wouldn't say that. They come in these big cardboard flats, wrapped in that thick plastic shrink wrap that you can never quite tear open with your fingers. You always need a pocket knife. I used to carry a Swiss Army knife everywhere I went. The red one with the little toothpick and tweezers tucked into the handle. I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're no longer enjoying things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things? I... I don't know. I guess I haven't really been doing much of anything lately to know if I enjoy it or not. I used to like bowling. I was in a league on Thursday nights down at Pinewood Lanes. We had a good team, too. The Pinbusters, we called ourselves. We even had matching shirts made up. Blue with white collars. \n\nMy buddy Frank, he was the anchor. He could throw a hook that would just curve right into the pocket every time. It was a beautiful thing to watch. He used a 16-pound ball, Brunswick, black as pitch. He polished that thing like it was a classic car. Speaking of classic cars, there was a guy in our neighborhood who restored an old '57 Chevy Bel Air. Cherry red with the white fins. He'd spend every weekend out in his driveway, buffing the chrome. I always wanted a car like that. Had a Ford Pinto once, back in the seventies. Terrible car. The heater never worked right, and you always felt like you were taking your life in your hands if you got rear-ended. Remember that? They had to recall them because the gas tanks would explode. My wife made me sell it. She was always practical like that. She kept all our receipts in these little manila envelopes, categorized by month...\n\nI'm sorry, what did you ask me? Did you want to know about the bowling league?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the day?\n\nPatient: Sad or empty? I don't really think so. I wouldn't use the word empty. Speaking of empty, though, you should see the loading dock at work by the end of my shift. I fill orders over at the warehouse, you know, and we just end up with absolute mountains of empty cardboard boxes. \n\nThe cardboard baler is always breaking down on us. The maintenance guy, Jimmy, he's supposed to fix it, but he usually just kicks the side of the machine and slaps a piece of duct tape on the safety latch. Jimmy used to be in the Navy. Stationed out in San Diego, he told me once. He's got this anchor tattoo on his forearm, but it just looks like a blurry blue blob now. I guess tattoos do that when you get older, the ink just kind of spreads out under your skin. My uncle Arthur had a hula girl on his bicep, and by the time he was my age, it looked like a head of cabbage. He was a real character, Arthur. Played the accordion. He used to play at these big Polish weddings on the weekends, polka music, everyone dancing and sweating... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Did you ask about my uncle?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going back to work?\n\nPatient: Anxious about work? Yeah, I guess you could say that. I mean, that's why I'm here, really. Ever since they changed my hours at the warehouse and put in that new computer inventory system, I just feel like I'm falling apart. I'm missing my shifts, I haven't been to my church commitments in weeks, and I'm snapping at my wife and kids. Some mornings I just sit there on the edge of the bed in my work boots, staring at the floor, wondering if I can even face the place anymore. \n\nThose work boots, they're Red Wings. Good, sturdy boots. I bought them at this little shoe store downtown that's been there since the fifties. The guy who runs it, Mr. Henderson, he still uses one of those old metal foot measurers, you know the ones? The Brannock device, I think it's called. You step on it and he slides the little metal pieces to check your width and length. Nobody does that anymore. Now you just go to a big box store and guess your size. It's a real shame. Mr. Henderson always throws in a free pair of wool socks with every purchase, too. Gray wool with a red stripe at the top. They're great for the winter, keep your toes nice and toasty. I tried wearing them in the summer once, though, and my feet sweat so much I thought I was going to slide right out of my boots...\n\nWait, what were we talking about? Did you ask about my boots?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself staying away from certain situations or activities because you're afraid of failing or making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Afraid of making mistakes? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really avoid things because of that. I mean, everybody makes mistakes, right? Like last week, I was trying to fix the leaky faucet in the downstairs bathroom. Just a simple washer replacement, or so I thought. I went to the hardware store—the one on Elm Street, not the big Home Depot out on the highway. The guy at the local place, he knows his stuff. He's got these little bins filled with every kind of screw and washer you could ever imagine. \n\nAnyway, I bought the wrong size washer. Brought it home, took the whole faucet apart, and realized it didn't fit. Water was spraying everywhere. I had to run down to the basement to shut off the main valve. The basement always smells a little musty, especially after it rains. We had a sump pump put in back in '98 when we got that terrible flood. The water came right up to the bottom step. Ruined a bunch of old photo albums my wife had stored in cardboard boxes. She was devastated. You know, you really shouldn't store things in cardboard if there's any chance of water. Plastic bins are the way to go. They have those ones with the snap-on lids now, very secure. \n\nI'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What were you asking me about? Was it the plumbing?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things at work even when you're not there?\n\nPatient: Worrying about work when I'm at home? No, I wouldn't say I do that. Once I clock out, it's out of my mind. Speaking of clocking out, we actually still use those old paper punch cards at the warehouse. You slide it in the slot and it goes *ka-chunk*, you know? It's a very satisfying sound. \n\nThey keep talking about bringing in one of those fancy fingerprint scanners, but I don't know how that would work for me. My fingerprints are practically gone. Just worn completely smooth from handling all those boxes and packing tape for years. My wife used to joke that I had hands like fine-grit sandpaper. She bought me this expensive hand cream once to soften them up. It came in this heavy glass pump bottle, smelled like... I think it was lavender and oatmeal? Anyway, I knocked the darn thing off the bathroom sink the second day I had it. Shattered everywhere. Getting glass out of tile grout is a nightmare, let me tell you. I had to drag the heavy Hoover vacuum upstairs. It's one of those old metal ones from the eighties, weighs an absolute ton, but it could suck a golf ball through a garden hose. Not like the cheap plastic ones they make now... \n\nI'm sorry, I completely lost my train of thought. Were you asking about my vacuum?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about things even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Worrying when there's no reason? No, not really. I mean, if there's nothing to worry about, I don't worry. I've always been pretty level-headed that way, you know? Like my grandfather used to say, don't borrow trouble. \n\nHe was a smart man, my grandfather. Worked on the railroad for forty-five years. He had this beautiful pocket watch, a Hamilton. Gold-plated, with the Roman numerals on the face. He used to let me wind it when I was a kid. You had to be real gentle with the stem, just turn it until you felt a little resistance. If you over-wound it, you'd snap the mainspring, and then you'd have to take it down to the jeweler on Main Street. Mr. Peterson, I think his name was. He wore this little magnifying glass on his eye—a loupe, they call it. Looked like a monocle. He had hands as steady as a surgeon. He fixed my wife's engagement ring once when a prong got bent. It's a modest ring, just a small diamond, but she loves it. We picked it out together at a pawn shop back in '71. Paid fifty dollars for it. Fifty dollars was a lot of money back then, let me tell you. I was working at a gas station at the time, pumping gas and checking oil. Full service, they called it. You don't see that anymore. Now you freeze your hands off in the winter trying to get the pump to work...\n\nWait, I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Was it about my grandfather?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Weight change? Hmm, no, I haven't really noticed anything like that. My clothes still fit about the same, I suppose. I buy mostly those trousers with the elastic in the waistband now anyway. They're just more comfortable when you're bending and lifting boxes all day. \n\nI actually get them from this catalog that comes in the mail. It's called Haband, I think. They always have these deals where you can get three pairs for twenty dollars, and they throw in a free belt. The belts aren't real leather, of course. They're that sort of shiny plastic material that cracks if you bend it too much. But they do the job. I used to wear real leather belts back when I was younger. Had this beautiful hand-tooled one with a silver buckle. Bought it at a leather shop down in New Mexico when my wife and I took a road trip out west in the seventies. We drove this old Ford station wagon with the wood paneling on the side. The air conditioning broke somewhere in Texas, and we had to drive with the windows down all the way to Albuquerque. The wind noise was so loud you couldn't even hear the radio. Not that we could get any good stations out there anyway, just static and farm reports...\n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I lost my train of thought. What were we talking about? Was it the road trip?\n\nDoctor: Do you often fear that something bad is going to happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Fear that something bad is going to happen? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't sit around waiting for the sky to fall, if that's what you mean. I just take things one day at a time. \n\nMy neighbor, though, Mrs. Higgins, she's a different story. She's always convinced a hurricane is coming, even in January. She keeps her pantry stocked with those giant cans of baked beans and peaches. You know, the industrial-sized ones they use in school cafeterias? I helped her carry some in from her car last week. Boy, those things are heavy. They come in these big cardboard flats, wrapped in that thick plastic shrink wrap that you can never quite tear open with your fingers. You always need a pocket knife. I used to carry a Swiss Army knife everywhere I went. The red one with the little toothpick and tweezers tucked into the handle. I lost the tweezers years ago, probably trying to pull a splinter out of my thumb when I was building the deck out back. I used treated pine for that deck. The guy at the lumber yard said it would last twenty years, but it started rotting after ten. Had to tear the whole thing down and start over. I rented one of those big crowbars, a pry bar, I think they call it. The thing was solid iron, painted bright yellow...\n\nI'm sorry, I've gone off track again, haven't I? Were you asking about my neighbor's pantry?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about everyday things, even when there's no obvious reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Excessively? No, no, I don't think so. I mean, you asked me this earlier, didn't you? About worrying when there's no reason? It's the same thing, really. I don't fret over every little thing. There's enough real things to handle without making up new ones. \n\nLike the boxes at work. I'm a stocker down at the warehouse, you know. I just turned 74, but I still go in. You have to pay attention to the labels, especially the ones with the barcodes. They changed the system last month, and now all the barcodes have these tiny little squares in them instead of the straight lines. QR codes, the young kid on the forklift called them. He's a nice kid, Jason. Wears his hat backwards. He's going to the community college, taking classes on computers. I took a few classes myself, years ago. Just some college, nothing fancy. Business administration, they called it. We had to use these big typewriters, IBM Selectrics. You remember those? They had that little metal ball that spun around and smacked the paper. Made a terrible racket, like a machine gun going off in the classroom. The ribbon was always getting jammed, and you'd get black ink all over your fingers trying to fix it. I used to use this special soap to get it off, Boraxo, I think it was called. Came in a cardboard can with a metal spout...\n\nWait, hold on. I'm sorry, doctor. I'm doing it again. What was the question? Something about the warehouse?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling anxious or worried for no specific reason?\n\nPatient: Anxious for no reason? Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. Like I just said a minute ago, no. I don't get worked up over nothing. I just go to work, do my job, eat my lunch, and come home. \n\nSpeaking of lunch, they actually took the microwave out of the break room at the warehouse on Tuesday. Said it was a fire hazard because somebody burned a bag of popcorn in it. Now I have to bring my soup in a thermos if I want it hot. I've got this old Stanley thermos, the heavy green metal kind. I think I bought it at a hardware store back in '92. They really don't make them like that anymore. The new ones are all plastic and they don't keep the heat past ten in the morning. My wife used to make this chicken noodle soup from scratch to put in there, with the thick egg noodles. She'd roll the dough out right on the kitchen counter, get flour all over the floor. We had this linoleum flooring back then, sort of a pale yellow with little flecks of brown in it. It was supposed to look like stone, I guess, but it just looked like dirty linoleum. I took a mop to it every Sunday with Pine-Sol. The smell of Pine-Sol always reminds me of Sunday mornings. We'd listen to the radio while I mopped...\n\nOh, for heaven's sake, I'm rambling again. I'm sorry, my mind just wanders off. What were we talking about? Was it the microwave?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what was going on in your life right before you started feeling like you were falling apart?\n\nPatient: Right before? Well, before all of this, I was keeping my routine and my responsibilities together just fine. I had my schedule, and I stuck to it. But then the warehouse changed my hours and brought in that new inventory system I was telling you about. It was within the first few weeks after they did all that when I really just... started coming apart. \n\nIt was the new scanners for that system that really threw me. They look like little plastic phaser guns from Star Trek. I used to watch Star Trek every Thursday night. The original one, you know, with William Shatner and Leonard Nimoy. We had this heavy wooden console television set back then. It was a Zenith, I think. Real wood veneer on the sides, with the speakers built right into the front covered by that scratchy cloth. The thing weighed an absolute ton and took up half the wall in the den. You had to physically get up off the couch and turn this big clunky dial if you wanted to change the channel. Not that there was much to change to, just channels 3, 5, and 8. Sometimes we'd try to get the UHF channels, but you had to mess with the rabbit ear antennas for twenty minutes. I used to wrap aluminum foil on the tips to get a better signal so we could watch the baseball games. My wife absolutely hated the foil. She said it made the living room look like a junkyard. She was very particular about how the house looked, always keeping the vacuum tracks perfectly straight on the carpet. She had this heavy old Hoover upright vacuum with a headlight on the front of it... \n\nOh, Lord, there I go again. I'm so sorry, doctor. I completely lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Something about the warehouse schedule?"
